From Six Functors Formalisms to Derived Motivic Measures
Joshua F. Lieber

Abstract
. In this paper, we generally describe a method of taking an abstract six functors formalism in the sense of Khan or Cisinski-Déglise, and outputting a derived motivic measure in the sense of Campbell-Wolfson-Zakharevich. In particular, we use this framework to define a lifting of the Gillet-Soulé motivic measure.



Conventions: Throughout this paper,
k
will always refer to a perfect field, and
R
will refer to an arbitrary commutative ring. Furthermore, given a base scheme
S
, the category
{\textbf{Var}}_{S}
of varieties over
S
will simply be the category of finite type separated schemes over
S
(we do not require our varieties to be reduced). Importantly, unless otherwise stated, our ambient
\infty
-cosmos
[
41
]
will be that of Kan complex-enriched categories, the so-called fibrant
S
-categories of Toën-Vezzozi
[
47
]
. This is because we will be working with commutative diagrams directly for much of the work, and it is easier to prove commutativity in a model with strict horizontal composition. That said, we may invoke comparison between the K-theory of
S
-categories and that of Quasicategories developed in
[
8
]
.

1.
Introduction

This paper was born out of a desire to construct a lift of the Gillet-Soulé motivic measure. While originally more limited in scope, its current iteration synthesizes a number of different recent developments in algebraic geometry and homotopy theory with an eye towards constructing meaningful derived motivic measures from six functors formalism.

One of the unusual features of this construction is that we are using something quite rare—namely, a six functors formalism (a highly structured object) to define a map of K-theory spectra (much less structure ultimately). That said, neither object is particularly approachable in and of itself, and our hope is that each can be used to study the other.

We begin the paper with a brief exploration of the three main types of categories we will require for the constructions that follow. These are Waldhausen categories, a now classical type of relative category used as a particularly general setting of K-theory; SW-categories, a more recently developed analogue of Waldhausen categories used to describe and categorify cutting and pasting data; and stable
\infty
-categories, a recent
\infty
-categorical enrichment of the notion of triangulated category which simplifies the definition considerably and makes many constructions (such as gluing and taking cones) functorial that are simply not on the level of triangulated categories. Note that we will not discuss the formalisms of model categories or DG/spectral categories in this work because they are only briefly touched upon. The motivated reader is referred to the phenomenal book by Hovey
[
27
]
for model categories and the wonderful introduction by Toën
[
45
]
for DG categories (spectral categories are a focal point of the groundbreaking paper
[
8
]
).

We will then briefly look at how to extract K-theory from each of these types of categories. The one novel aspect of this section will be to demonstrate how to meaningfully extend the definition of weakly W-exact functor
[
12
]
from the original setting (in which one compares an SW-category to a Waldhausen category) to a comparison between SW-categories and pointed, finitely homotopy cocomplete
\infty
-categories (as before incarnated as fibrant
S
-categories):

Theorem 1.1
.

Given a weakly W-exact functor
F:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
, where
{\mathcal{C}}
is an SW-category and
{\mathcal{A}}
is a pointed finitely homotopy cocomplete
\infty
-category, it may be composed with the Yoneda embedding
{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})
to obtain a weakly W-exact functor
\iota\circ F:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})
to the Waldhausen
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})
which yields a map on K-theory
K({\mathcal{C}})\to K({\mathcal{A}})
. This construction is functorial in exact functors both of SW-categories and of pointed finitely homotopy cocomplete
\infty
-categories.

The interesting thing here is that in some sense, this is not even an extension of the definition, as given a particularly nice
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{A}}
(in a sense to be defined in a later section) that embeds into a simplicial model category, we may functorially assign it a Waldhausen category
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})
such that
K({\mathcal{A}})\simeq K({\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}}))\simeq K(N^{hc}({\mathcal{A}}^{cf}))
, where the last term is the K-theory of the homotopy coherent nerve of the cofibrant-fibrant objects in
{\mathcal{A}}
, which is the corresponding quasicategory under the homotopy coherent nerve functor. It just so happens that in all cases we consider, the necessary niceness conditions will be met.

From here, we go on to describe the
\infty
-categorical six functors formalism outlined in Khan
[
29
]
, alternately titled the theory of
(*,\#,\otimes)
-formalisms satisfying the Voevodsky criteria or the theory of motivic
\infty
-categories. Upon introducing the basic setup, we go on to show that for a motivic
\infty
-category
{\mathbb{D}}
valued over
{\mathcal{S}}
, where
{\mathcal{S}}
is some appropriate subcategory of schemes, given any excellent geometrically unibranch scheme
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
and a variety
f:X\to S
over
S
, the assignment
X\mapsto f_{*}f^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})
, where
\mathbf{1}_{S}
is the tensor unit
{\mathbb{D}}(S)
, upgrades to a weakly W-exact functor in the following way.

Theorem 1.2
.

Suppose that
{\mathbb{D}}
satisfies one of the following two sets of conditions:

•

The four functors preserve constructible objects when given input a seperable morphism of finite type (note that compactness is trivially preserved by tensor)

•

The six functors preserve constructible objects over Noetherian quasi-excellent schemes of finite dimension with respect to morphisms of finite type (in other words, for any finite type morphism
f:X\to S
with target Noetherian quasi-excellent of finite dimension, the four functors preserve constructible objects, while tensor and
{\rm Hom}
preserve constructible objects over
S
)

Then, given a scheme
S
(assumed to be Noetherian quasi-excellent of finite dimension if
{\mathbb{D}}
satisfies the second condition in particular), there is a weakly W-exact functor

M^{c}_{{\mathbb{D}}(S)}:{\textbf{Var}}_{S}\to{\mathbb{D}}_{{\text{cons}}}(S)

sending each variety (smooth or otherwise)
(X\overset{f}{\to}S)\in{\textbf{Var}}_{S}
to
M^{c}_{{\mathbb{D}}(S)}(X):=f_{*}f^{!}\mathbf{1}_{S}
.

This theorem forms the core of the paper, as it allows us to conclude the existence of a derived motivic measure:

Corollary 1.3
.

Suppose
{\mathbb{D}}
sastisfies one of the two conditions of the above theorem. Then, given a scheme
S
(assumed to be Noetherian quasi-excellent of finite dimension if
{\mathbb{D}}
satisfies the second set of conditions), one obtains a map of K-theory spectra

K(M^{c}_{{\mathbb{D}}(S)}):K({\textbf{Var}}_{S})\to K({\mathbb{D}}_{{\text{cons}}}(S)).

From here, we apply our construction to the motivic
\infty
-category of Beilinson motives
{\textbf{DM}}_{B}
, and show that the construction restricts to a spectral lift of the Gillet-Soulé motivic measure, thus demonstrating our stated intent.

Theorem 1.4
.

Considering a perfect base field
k
and rational coefficients, the map

K(M^{c}_{k}):K({\textbf{Var}}_{k})\to K({\textbf{DM}}_{B}^{c}(k))

yields a derived lift of the Gillet-Soulé motivic measure.

This paper concludes with a brief discussion of another approach to lifting the Gillet-Soulé motivic measure (which is almost surely equivalent), before mentioning some future directions this work might take.

2.
Waldhausen Categories, SW-Categories, Stable
\infty
-Categories and the K-Theory of Varieties

2.1.
Relevant Categorical Definitions and Examples

In this section, we will outline the different notions of
1
or
\infty
-categories that are needed in this paper, as well as elucidate how to extract K-theory from each of them.

Definition 2.1
.

A
Waldhausen category

({\mathcal{C}},{\textbf{cof}},{\mathcal{W}})
consists of the data of a category
{\mathcal{C}}
with two distinguished classes of morphisms:
cofibrations

cof
(elements of which are denoted
\hookrightarrow
) and
weak equivalences

{\mathcal{W}}
(elements of which are denoted
\overset{\sim}{\to}
) which are required to satisfy the following axioms:

(1)

All isomorphisms are cofibrations

(2)

{\mathcal{C}}
has a zero object, and for any zero object
0\in{\mathcal{C}}
and any
X\in{\mathcal{C}}
, the map
0\to X
is a cofibration

(3)

Cofibrations are stable under pushout, so given any cofibration
X\hookrightarrow Y
and any morphism
X\to Y
, the map
Y\to Y\cup_{X}Z
is a cofibration

(4)

All isomorphisms are weak equivalences

(5)

Weak equivalences are closed under composition and hence form a subcategory

(6)

Given a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the vertical arrows are weak equivalences and the horizontal arrows of the right square are cofibrations, one has that the induced map

Y\cup_{X}Z\overset{\sim}{\to}Y^{\prime}\cup_{X^{\prime}}Z^{\prime}

is a weak equivalence as well.

There is also a natural notion of functor between Waldhausen categories.

Definition 2.2
.

A functor
F:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
between two Waldhausen categories is
exact
if it preserves cofibrations, weak equivalences, and finite (homotopy) colimits. The category of Waldhausen categories and exact functors will be denoted
Wald
.

While Waldhausen categories provided one of the earliest settings for the algebraic K-theory of categories (preceded only by exact categories), in recent years, several other (often related) frameworks have been used. One of the most important (for this and many other reasons) is that of stable quasicategories.

Definition 2.3
.

Suppose that one has a pointed quasicategory
{\mathcal{C}}
. Given any morphism
X\to Y
in
{\mathcal{C}}
, its
kernel
and
cokernel
are defined, if they exist, by the homotopy cartesian and cocartesian squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Ker}{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Coker}{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

respectively.

In general, an arbitrary commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is called a
triangle
. If it is cartesian, it is called an
exact triangle
and if it is cocartesian, it is called a
coexact triangle
.

Definition 2.4
.

A quasicategory
{\mathcal{C}}
is
stable
if it satisfies the following conditions:

•

{\mathcal{C}}
is pointed (i.e. has a zero object)

•

Every morphism in
{\mathcal{C}}
has a kernel and a cokernel

•

Every exact triangle is coexact and every coexact triangle is exact.

An
exact functor

F:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
between stable quasicategories is one which preserves finite colimits.

This extremely simple definition belies its incredible depth. In particular, the homotopy category of a stable quasicategory is a triangulated category, and essentially every important example of triangulated categories arises as such a homotopy category. We will not describe the theory of stable quasicategories here (a comprehensive guide is that of
[
35
]
).

Before continuing, we note the following proposition/definition

Proposition 2.5
.

There is a Quillen equivalence between the model category of simplicial sets equipped with the Joyal model structure and the model category of simplicial categories equipped with the Bergner model structure:

(\mathfrak{C}\dashv N^{hc}):\textbf{sSet}_{\text{Joyal}}\rightleftarrows\textbf{sSetCat}_{\text{Bergner}}.

The right adjoint is known as the
homotopy coherent nerve
and takes Kan-enriched categories to quasicategories. Furthermore, it fits into a strictly commutative triangle

In other words, the classical nerve of a category may be factored into the inclusion of categories into simplicial categories as simplicial categories with discrete
{\rm Hom}
simplicial sets followed by the homotopy coherent nerve.

Proof.

See
[
6
]
section 7.4.
∎

This allows us a direct comparison between our chosen model of
\infty
-categories and the one which is most often used.

Definition 2.6
.

A stable
\infty
-category (incarnated as a fibrant
S
-category) will be one which maps under the homotopy coherent nerve to a stable quasicategory. It should be noted that these will be precisely the
S
-categories for which the above three axioms hold, but the word homotopy is inserted in front of the word (co)cartesian in the definition of (co)exact triangles.

An
exact functor

F:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
between two stable
\infty
-categories will be one which preserves finite homotopy colimits.

The last class of categories we will look at are so-called SW-categories and their precursors. These categories were introduced as a way of categorifying the notion of subtraction present in settings such as decomposing varieties into open and closed complementary subsets and decomposition of polytopes. In particular, they allow us a method of lifting universal Euler characteristics to the level of spectra, and will be extremely important later in the paper. To define SW-categories, we must first define a few prerequisite categorical structures along the way. It should also be noted that there are other categorical frameworks that deal with the concept of subtraction and scissors congruence —most notably the notion of an assembler category first introduced by Inna Zakharevich in
[
51
]
. Assembler categories are more natural to define, but are slightly less easy to map out of (into targets such as Waldhausen categories or stable
\infty
-categories, for example). For this latter reason, our preferred setting is that of SW-categories. It should also be noted that for our purposes, subtractive categories are really enough, as our ”weak equivalences” will merely be isomorphisms.

Definition 2.7
.

A
pre-subtractive category

{\mathcal{C}}
is a category equipped with two wide subcategories
{\textbf{cof}}({\mathcal{C}})
and
{\textbf{comp}}({\mathcal{C}})
referred to as
cofibrations
and
complements
(morphisms in
{\textbf{cof}}({\mathcal{C}})
are denoted by
\hookrightarrow
and morphisms in
{\textbf{comp}}({\mathcal{C}})
are denoted by
\overset{\circ}{\to}
) and equipped with a subclass
{\textbf{sub}}({\mathcal{C}})
of diagrams of the form
Z\hookrightarrow X\overset{\circ}{\leftarrow}Y
referred to as
subtraction sequences
. These are all required to satisfy the following axioms:

•

{\mathcal{C}}
has an initial object (often referred to via
\emptyset
in practice)

•

{\mathcal{C}}
has finite coproducts, and for every
X,Y\in{\mathcal{C}}
, one has that
X\to X\coprod Y
is both a cofibration and a complement

•

Pullbacks along cofibrations and complements exist and are cofibrations and complements respectively

•

For every
X,Y\in{\mathcal{C}}
, one has
X\hookrightarrow X\coprod Y\overset{\circ}{\leftarrow}Y\in{\textbf{sub}}({\mathcal{C}})

•

Every cofibration
X\hookrightarrow Y
participates in a subtraction sequence
Z\hookrightarrow X\overset{\circ}{\leftarrow}Y
which is unique up to unique isomorphism. We denote this unique
Y
by
X-Z
. The same statement is true in reverse for every complement

•

Any cartesian square of cofibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}

can be completed into a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X-Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W-Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y-Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W-X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

where
S:=(W-X)-(Y-Z)\cong(W-Y)-(X-Z)
in such a way that every row and column will be a subtraction sequence, the bottom row and rightmost column are uniquely determined once choices of the complement are made, and the bottom-right square will also be cartesian. The dual statement holds for cartesian squares of complements

•

Subtraction is stable under pullback, or in other words, given any subtraction sequence
Z\hookrightarrow X\overset{\circ}{\leftarrow}Y
and any morphism
W\to X
in
{\mathcal{C}}
, one has that
Z\times_{X}W\hookrightarrow W\overset{\circ}{\leftarrow}Y\times_{X}W
is also a subtraction sequence

We will often merely refer to a pre-subtractive category as
{\mathcal{C}}
instead of specifying all of the attendant data. The most important examples of pre-subtractive categories for us are actually subtractive categories, as discussed immediately below.

Definition 2.8
.

A
subtractive category
is a pre-subtractive category
{\mathcal{C}}
which additionally satisfies the following axioms:

•

The pushout of a diagram in which both legs are cofibrations exists and satisfies base-change (the created maps are also cofibrations). Furthermore, cocartesian diagrams of this form are cartesian as well

•

Given a cartesian diagram of cofibrations

\hbox to81.59pt{\vbox to46.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.79701pt\lower-23.35274pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

the natural map
X\coprod_{Z}Y\hookrightarrow W
must also be a cofibration

•

Given a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

in which all the columns are subtraction sequences and all of the horizontal morphisms are cofibrations, the sequence
X^{\prime}\coprod_{W^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}\hookrightarrow W\coprod_{W}Y\overset{\circ}{\leftarrow}X^{\prime\prime}\coprod_{W^{\prime\prime}}Y^{\prime\prime}
is a subtraction sequence

In particular, it is proven in
[
12
]
proposition 3.28 that given a base scheme
S
, the categories
{\textbf{Var}}_{S}
and
{\textbf{Sch}}_{S}
of
S
-varieties and
S
-schemes, respectively, have the structure of subtractive categories with cofibrations being closed immersions, complements being open immersions, and subtractions given by decomposition of varieties into complementary closed and open subvarieties.

Definition 2.9
.

An
exact functor

F:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
between subtractive categories is a functor which satisfies the following properties:

•

F
preserves zero objects

•

F
preserves subtraction sequences

•

F
preserves cocartesian squares

The category of subtractive categories and exact functors is denoted
SubCat
.

As one can imagine, the inclusion functor
{\textbf{Var}}_{S}\hookrightarrow{\textbf{Sch}}_{S}
is in fact an exact functor of subtractve categories.

Now, we have all that is needed for the current paper, but if we happen to want a notion of weak equivalence that ”plays nicely” with our other categories, we may enlarge our definition somewhat.

Definition 2.10
.

An
SW-category
is a subtractive category
{\mathcal{C}}
equipped with an additional distinguished wide subcategory
w{\mathcal{C}}
of
weak equivalences
(arrows in which are denoted
\overset{\sim}{\to}
) subject to the following conditions:

•

w{\mathcal{C}}
contains all isomorphisms

•

Given a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

in which horizontal arrows are cofibrations and vertical arrows are weak equivalences, one has that the resulting map
X^{\prime}\coprod_{W^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}X\coprod_{W}Y
is a weak equivalence

•

Weak equivalences respect subtraction, or in other words, given any commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

we may complete it into a commutative diagram

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Definition 2.11
.

A functor
F:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
of SW-categories is
exact
if it is exact as a functor of subtractive categories and preserves weak equivalences. The category of SW-categories and weak equivalences will be denoted
SW-Cat
.

If it seems thus far that we have been specifying a good deal more data than in the Waldhausen case, you are correct. In truth, cutting and pasting/subtraction data does not play particularly nicely with homotopy, and several strong axioms are needed to ensure that we can say anything at all. Luckily, in spite of the strength of the axioms introduced, the most important examples, namely
{\textbf{Var}}_{S}
and
{\textbf{Sch}}_{S}
, satisfy these strong axioms, allowing us to define K-theory, among other things.

2.2.
Maps from Waldhausen and SW
1
-categories into stable
\infty
-categories

In the current subsection, we will only develop as much of the comparison theory as is needed for this paper. All of this can be developed more generally, as has already been done in the case of Waldhausen
\infty
-categories by Clark Barwick. That said, it ought to be possible to replace the
1
-categorical theory of SW-categories with an
\infty
-categorical analogue, although we do not do this here. Indeed, this generality would be unnecessary for our current setting, as the sub-
\infty
-category of derived prestacks generated by algebraic varieties is discrete (or in other words, forms a
1
-category). All our
1
-categories will be implicitly included into
\infty
-categories (in other words, we are making use of the inclusion of
1
-categories into simplicial categories as the simplicial categories with discrete
{\rm Hom}
simplicial sets). Furthermore, for this subsection in particular, we will make the assumption that all of our
\infty
-categories are small as a precaution to prevent swindles and ensure everything is well defined.

Before we begin discussing maps between the different types of
\infty
-categories we will be using, let us say a little bit about how to extract K-theory from each one.

Definition 2.12
.

Define
\text{Ar}[n]
to be the full subcategory of
[n]\times[n]
consisting of
(i,j)\in[n]\times[n]
with
i\leq j
and
\tilde{\text{Ar}}[n]
to be the full subcategory of
[n]^{op}\times[n]
consisting of
(i,j)\in[n]^{op}\times[n]
with
i\leq j
. The former is used in the construction of Waldhausen’s K-theory and the K-theory of pointed
\infty
-categories (specifically, stable
\infty
-categories), while the latter is used in the construction of K-theory of SW-categories.

Waldhausen categories were of course first defined as a general setting for algebraic K-theory. Waldhausen’s
S_{\bullet}
-construction is quite well-known at this point, so we will omit the basic variation here (see, for example,
[
50
]
or
[
49
]
). That said, we will make relatively heavy use of a variation known as the
S^{\prime}_{\bullet}
-construction which is defined for Waldhausen categories which admit a functorial factorization of any morphism into a cofibration followed by a weak equivalence. We recall briefly that, in such a Waldhausen category, a homotopy cocartesian square is weakly equivalent via a zigzag of equivalences to a pushout square with one leg a cofibration and a weak cofibration is a map equivalent to a cofibration by a zigzag of weak equivalences.

Definition 2.13
.

We begin by defining
S^{\prime}_{n}{\mathcal{C}}
to be the full subcategory of
\text{Fun}(\text{Ar}[n],{\mathcal{C}})
spanned by functors
F:\text{Ar}[n]\to{\mathcal{C}}
such that

•

F(i,i)
is a zero object for all
i
between
0
and
n

•

F(i,j)\to F(i,k)
is a weak cofibration for any
i\leq j\leq k

•

Whenever
i<j<k
, one has that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(j,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(j,k)}

is homotopy cocartesian

These categories bundle together to form a simplicial object in categories
S^{\prime}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{C}}
. This construction is functorial in exact functors.

Each
S^{\prime}_{n}{\mathcal{C}}
may naturally be given the structure of a Waldhausen category itself, so we may define a multisimplicial category
(S^{\prime}_{\bullet})^{n}{\mathcal{C}}
by iterating the construction at every simplicial level
n
times. We may then define the K-theory spectrum
K({\mathcal{C}})
of this Waldhausen category with
n
-th space
|w(S^{\prime}_{\bullet})^{n}{\mathcal{C}}|
. We do not use a different notation for this K-theory space, as it coincides with the standard Waldhausen K-theory in the case we described before.

We will also define K-theory for general pointed finitely cocomplete quasicategories. Given any pointed finitely cocomplete quasicategory
{\mathcal{A}}
, one can define K-theory in the following way.

Definition 2.14
.

We begin by defining
\text{Gap}([n],{\mathcal{A}})
to be the full subcategory of
\text{Fun}(\text{Ar}[n],{\mathcal{A}})
spanned by functors
F:\text{Ar}[n]\to{\mathcal{A}}
such that

•

F(i,i)
is a zero object for all
i
between
0
and
n

•

Whenever
i<j<k
, one has that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(j,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(j,k)}

is cocartesian

Note that
\text{Gap}([n],{\mathcal{A}})
is always pointed and finitely cocomplete via
[
8
]
, and when
{\mathcal{A}}
is stable, it is as well.

Definition 2.15
.

Given
{\mathcal{A}}
pointed and finitely cocomplete (resp. stable), we define the simplicial pointed finitely cocomplete (resp. stable) quasicategory
S^{\infty}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{A}}
via
S^{\infty}_{n}{\mathcal{A}}:=\text{Gap}([n],{\mathcal{A}})
on objects, with face maps given by the composition of the arrows into and out of the objects on the
i
th row and column and degeneracy maps given by inserting a copy of the identity in position
i
on rows and columns. In particular, denoting by
\iota
the largest internal
\infty
-groupoid functor, this also yields a simplicial
\infty
-groupoid
\iota S^{\infty}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{A}}
.

Note that we can actually iterate the construction above to give a multisimplicial pointed finitely cocomplete (resp. stable)
\infty
-category
(S^{\infty}_{\bullet})^{n}{\mathcal{A}}
and corresponding multisimplicial
\infty
-groupoid
\iota(S^{\infty}_{\bullet})^{n}{\mathcal{A}}
.

Definition 2.16
.

Given a pointed finitely cocomplete (resp. stable)
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{A}}
, we define its
K-theory spectrum

K({\mathcal{A}})
as (the fibrant-cofibrant replacement of what) follows. For every
n
, the
n
th space in the spectrum is
|\iota(S^{\infty}_{\bullet})^{n}{\mathcal{A}}|
. Noting that
\text{Gap}([0],{\mathcal{A}})
is contractible and that
\text{Gap}([1],{\mathcal{A}})
is naturally equivalent to
{\mathcal{A}}
, we obtain a natural map
S^{1}\wedge|\iota{\mathcal{A}}|\to|S^{\infty}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{A}}|
, which induces all of our connecting maps.

This construction is natural in exact functors.

When restricted to small stable
\infty
-categories equipped with the Lurie tensor product, the above construction defines a symmetric monoidal functor to the category of spectra. In particular, this implies given a symmetric monoidal small
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{A}}
, that the K-theory spectrum
K({\mathcal{A}})
is a
{\mathbb{E}}_{\infty}
-ring spectrum.

We mostly introduce the notion of K-theory of quasicategories as a ”standard” K-theory of
\infty
-categories to compare against, simply because this K-theory has had many nice properties proved about it already. Before this, we introduce the K-theory of SW-categories and describe how to compare it to the K-theory of
\infty
-categories (incarnated as
S
-categories), which we also introduce.

Definition 2.17
.

Given any pointed finitely cocomplete
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{A}}
(considered as a Kan-enriched category), one may consider its category of pointed simplicial presheaves
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{A}})
equipped with the projective model structure. From here, one can left Bousfeld localize this category to form the simplicial model category
{\mathcal{P}}_{ex}({\mathcal{A}})
whose local objects are those functors
{\mathcal{A}}^{op}
to simplicial sets which commute with finite (homotopy) colimits. Finally, we can construct a Waldhausen subcategory
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})
of
{\mathcal{P}}_{ex}({\mathcal{A}})
which consists of those objects which are cofibrant and weakly equivalent to representable presheaves. From here, we may define
the K-theory spectrum of
{\mathcal{A}}
to be the the Waldhausen K-theory
K({\mathcal{A}}):=K({\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}}))
.

It is this definition of K-theory of
\infty
-categories that we will compare with the K-theory of SW-categories. Note that if one has a morphism
{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
of pointed, finitely homotopy cocomplete
\infty
-categories which commutes with the relevant structures (a so-called weakly exact functor), one obtains an exact morphism
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})\to{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{B}})
of Waldhausen categories by restricting the left Kan extension of our original morphism. In this way, we obtain a functor from pointed finitely homotopy cocomplete
\infty
-categories and weakly exact functors to (strongly saturated) Waldhausen categories and exact functors. This, in turn, may be composed with K-theory, which shows that the above definition of K-theory is functorial (
[
14
]
page 544).

This will allow us an easier way of defining SW-exact functors to
\infty
-categories without actually needing to enlarge our definition all that much.

Before we continue, let us begin by comparing the above two constructions on nice
\infty
-categories to ensure they coincide.

Theorem 2.18
.

Given a simplicial model category
{\mathcal{C}}
and a small full subcategory
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}
whose objects are all cofibrant, which admits all homotopy colimits, and whose underlying category inherits the structure of a Waldhausen category from the model structure on
{\mathcal{C}}
, one has equivalences

K({\mathcal{A}})\simeq K({\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}}))\simeq K(N^{hc}({\mathcal{A}}^{cf})),

where
{\mathcal{A}}^{cf}
is the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{A}}
generated by objects which are both cofibrant and fibrant in
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

This combines
[
8
]
Theorems 7.8 and 7.11 and Corollary 7.12.
∎

In particular, this will be true for all of our categories of interest, as any stable
S
-category embeds into a stable simplicial model category (see
[
46
]
page 789 paragraph 1).

Definition 2.19
.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{C}}
is an SW-category. In analogy with the Waldhausen case, we may define a simplicial object in categories
\tilde{S}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{C}}
as follows. We start by defining it as a simplicial set. For each
n
, define
\tilde{S}_{n}{\mathcal{C}}
to the full subcategory of
\text{Fun}(\tilde{\text{Ar}}[n],{\mathcal{C}})
on the functors
F:\tilde{\text{Ar}}[n]\to{\mathcal{C}}
satisfying the following conditions:

•

F(i,i)
is initial for any
i
between
0
and
n

•

Whenever
j<k
, one has
F(i,j)\to F(i,k)
is in
{\textbf{cof}}({\mathcal{C}})

•

Whenever
i<j<k
one has that

F(i,j)\rightarrow F(i,k)\leftarrow F(j,k)

is a subtraction sequence.

The face maps
d_{k}:\tilde{S}_{n}{\mathcal{C}}\to\tilde{S}_{n-1}{\mathcal{C}}
are given by deleting the
k
th row and column of the requisite functors from
\tilde{\text{Ar}}[n]
, and composing the appropriate morphisms to yield a functor from
\tilde{\text{Ar}}[n-1]
into
{\mathcal{C}}
. The degeneracy maps are given by inserting identity maps into the
i
th row and column. Furthermore, each
\tilde{S}_{n}{\mathcal{C}}
naturally has the structure of an SW-category (we can check whether a morphism of diagrams is a cofibration, complement, or weak equivalence object-wise), so we can iterate this construction to an
n
-ary simplicial structure
\tilde{S}^{n}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{C}}
.

Definition 2.20
.

Given an SW-category
{\mathcal{C}}
, one can define its
K-theory spectrum

K({\mathcal{C}})
as (the fibrant-cofibrant repacement of what) follows. Define the
n
th space of the spectrum to be
|w\tilde{S}_{\bullet}^{n}{\mathcal{C}}|
. By
[
12
]
corollary 4.17, one gets a natural map
|w{\mathcal{C}}|\to\Omega|w\tilde{S}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{C}}|
which, while not an equivalence, becomes one on higher levels, so we actually have a spectrum.

As an example,
|w\tilde{S}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{C}}|\overset{\sim}{\to}\Omega|w\tilde{S}_{\bullet}\tilde{S}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{C}}|
. The spectrum
K({\mathcal{C}})
also has the natural structure of a
{\mathbb{E}}_{\infty}
-ring spectrum whenever
{\mathcal{C}}
is equipped with a symmetric monoidal structure compatible with subtraction (
[
12
]
theorem 5.14). This construction defines a functor from SW-categories equipped with exact functors of SW-categories to spectra.

Definition 2.21
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be an SW-category and
{\mathcal{A}}
be a pointed homotopy cocomplete
\infty
-category. A
weakly W-exact functor

F:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
actually consists of a triple
(F_{!},F^{!},F_{w})
of functors such that

•

F_{!}
is a functor
F_{!}:{\textbf{cof}}({\mathcal{C}})\to{\mathcal{A}}
. We abbreviate
F_{!}(i)
to
i_{!}
for all cofibrations
i

•

F^{!}
is a functor
F^{!}:{\textbf{comp}}({\mathcal{C}})^{op}\to{\mathcal{A}}
. We abbreviate
F^{!}(j)
to
j^{!}
for all complement maps
j

•

F_{w}
is a functor
F_{w}:w{\mathcal{C}}\to\iota({\mathcal{A}})
. We abbreviate
F_{w}(f)
to
f_{w}
for all weak equivalences

•

For all objects
X\in{\mathcal{C}}
, one has
F_{!}(X)=F^{!}(X)=F_{w}(X)=:F(X)

•

Given a cartesian square in
{\mathcal{C}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}

with horizontal morphisms cofibrations and vertical morphisms complement maps, one gets a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(W)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime!}}

•

For all subtraction sequences
Z\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}X\overset{j}{\underset{\circ}{\leftarrow}}U
, one gets a homotopy cocartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(U)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{!}}

in
{\mathcal{A}}

•

For all commutative squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

with vertical morphisms weak equivalences and horizontal morphisms cofibrations, one gets a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(W)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}_{w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{!}}

in
{\mathcal{A}}
. One gets an analogous diagram if one replaces cofibrations with complements

Our reason for introducing such a map is that such a map induces a contractible space of maps of K-theory spectra.

Remark 2.22
.

Note that this is precisely the same as the definition of a weakly W-exact functor where the target is a Waldhausen category (
[
13
]
definition 2.17). If
F:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
is a weakly W-exact functor with target a Waldhausen category, it induces a map
K({\mathcal{F}}):K({\mathcal{C}})\to K({\mathcal{A}})
(
[
13
]
proposition 2.19). We make use of this in the following theorem.

Theorem 2.23
.

Given a weakly W-exact functor
F:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
, it may be composed with the Yoneda embedding
{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})
to obtain a weakly W-exact functor
\iota\circ F:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})
to the good Waldhausen
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})
which yields a map on K-theory
K({\mathcal{C}})\to K({\mathcal{A}})
. This construction is functorial in exact functors both of SW-categories and of pointed finitely homotopy cocomplete
\infty
-categories.

Proof.

Let us begin by noting that
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})
has a natural enrichment as a simplicial category which makes the restriction of the Yoneda embedding
\iota:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})
into a functor of simplicial categories (and indeed one which preserves finite homotopy colimits by construction). As such, the corresponding map
\iota\circ F
remains weakly W-exact. Furthermore, by construction, it must descend to a weakly W-exact map on the level of the underlying Waldhausen category
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{A}})
. Thus, one has a natural map on K-theory
K({\mathcal{C}})\to K({\mathcal{A}})
induced from
\iota\circ F
. Furthermore, one has that functoriality in weakly W-exact functors and weakly exact functors arises from the functoriality of the corresponding constructions on their respective K-theories.
∎

3.
The Six Functors Formalism and Derived Motivic Measures

3.1.
A Brief Description of the Generalized Six Functors Formalism

In this section, we will discuss some of the basic aspects of the
\infty
-categorical theory of six functors formalisms described by Adeel Khan in
[
29
]
. We will not go into detail regarding the proofs, and will only supply the details necessary for the sections that follow. It should be noted that none of this section is novel. Any interested reader is urged to peruse the stellar paper by Khan on the subject.
Before we begin, we will detail a few conventions.

For the rest of the section
{\mathcal{S}}
will refer to a category of classical Noetherian schemes over some fixed Noetherian base. While Khan’s original formalism works more generally for derived algebraic spaces, for the moment, we are only concerned with a more limited setup. Note that these stronger conditions imply, among other things, that
{\mathcal{S}}
is actually a
1
-category (a discrete
\infty
-category). Furthermore, we will assume that
{\mathcal{S}}
is such that for all
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
, one has

•

U\in{\mathcal{S}}
for every quasicompact open subspace
U\subseteq S

•

Z\in{\mathcal{S}}
for every closed subspace
Z\subseteq S

•

{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})\in{\mathcal{S}}
for every finite locally free sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}
on
S

We further fix a class of so-called
admissible
morphisms in
{\mathcal{S}}
, which we mandate to contain all open immersions and all projections
X\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n}\to X
, to be closed under composition and base change, and to satisfy 2-out-of-3. We denote by
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
the subcategory of admissible morphisms.

If we consider a presheaf of
\infty
-categories
{\mathbb{D}}^{*}
on
{\mathcal{S}}
, we will use the notation
{\mathbb{D}}(S):={\mathbb{D}}^{*}(S)
for every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
. Furthermore, for every morphism
f:X\to Y\in{\mathcal{S}}
, we will denote by

f^{*}:{\mathbb{D}}(Y)\to{\mathbb{D}}(X)

the inverse image
{\mathbb{D}}^{*}(f)
of
f
.

If, furthermore,
{\mathbb{D}}^{*}
takes values in presentable
\infty
-categories and colimit-preserving functors, we refer to
{\mathbb{D}}
as a
presheaf of presentable
\infty
-categories
. Note that this, in particular, means that
f^{*}
must admit a right adjoint, which we denote by
f_{*}
and call the direct image of
f
.

Finally, if
{\mathbb{D}}^{*}
further factors through symmetric monoidal presentable
\infty
-categories (presentable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories for which the tensor product commutes with colimits in each variable), we refer to
{\mathbb{D}}^{*}
as a
presheaf of symmetric monoidal presentable
\infty
-categories
. Given any
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
, we will let
\otimes:{\mathbb{D}}(S)\times{\mathbb{D}}(S)\to{\mathbb{D}}(S)
denote the monoidal product and
\mathbf{1}_{S}
denote the monoidal unit. Now, since
\otimes
commutes with colimits in each variable, it admits a right-adjoint internal hom bifunctor
\underline{{\rm Hom}}:{\mathbb{D}}(S)^{op}\times{\mathbb{D}}(S)\to{\mathbb{D}}(S)
. From now on, when we talk about this last scenario, we will simply omit the upper
*
from the notation unless we want to make it clear that we are referring to
{\mathbb{D}}
as presheaf with respect to
*
morphisms.

Definition 3.1
.

A
premotivic
\infty
-category
or
(*,\#,\otimes)
-formalism
on
({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}})
is a presheaf of symmetric monoidal presentable
\infty
-categories
{\mathbb{D}}
on
{\mathcal{S}}
which satisfies the following additional properties

•

For every morphism
f:T\to S
in
{\mathcal{A}}
, the inverse image functor admits a left-adjoint
f_{\#}:{\mathbb{D}}(T)\to{\mathbb{D}}(S)
which we call the
\#
-direct image

•

f_{\#}:{\mathbb{D}}(T)\to{\mathbb{D}}(S)
is a morphism of
{\mathbb{D}}(S)
-modules, where we note that
{\mathbb{D}}(T)
has a natural
{\mathbb{D}}(S)
-module structure via the symmetric monoidal functor
f^{*}
. In other words,
{\mathbb{D}}
satisfies the
projection formula
with respect to
\#
-direct images

•

{\mathbb{D}}
satisfies
admissible base change
for
\#
-direct image. In other words, given any cartesian diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
p
and
q
admissible, then there is a natural equivalence of functors

{\text{Ex}}_{\#}^{*}:q_{\#}g^{*}\overset{\sim}{\to}f^{*}p_{\#}

•

Given any finite family
S_{\alpha}
in
{\mathcal{S}}
, the induced functor

{\mathbb{D}}(\amalg_{\alpha}S_{\alpha})\to\Pi_{\alpha}{\mathbb{D}}(S_{\alpha})

from inclusion is an equivalence. In other words,
{\mathbb{D}}
satisfies additivity

Generally speaking, when talking about premotivic
\infty
-categories on
{\mathcal{S}}
, if admissible morphisms are not specified as part of the data, then we are making the assumption that the admissible morphisms are simply the smooth morphisms in
{\mathcal{S}}
(or
{\mathcal{A}}=Sm
).

Remark 3.2
.

Note that a
(*,\#,\otimes)
-formalism is the same as a premotivic
\infty
-category in the sense of Cisinski-Déglise (hence the conflation of the two terms above), so we may directly import the notions of premotivic morphism and premotivic adjunction to this setting (indeed, we note that premotivic stable-symmetric monoidal model categories are a direct realization of our current situation in more classical language when specified to the stable case).

We will delay discussion of examples until the following section.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
{\mathbb{D}}
be a
(*,\#,\otimes)
-formalism on
({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}})
, and take
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
and a finite locally free sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}
on
S
. Denote the total space
{\mathbb{V}}_{S}({\mathcal{E}})
by
E
, and let
p:E\to S
be the projection and
s:S\to E
be the zero section. Supposing that
p
is admissible, define the
Thom twist

\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
to be the endofunctor on
{\mathbb{D}}(S)
given by

{\mathcal{F}}\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle:=p_{\#}s_{*}({\mathcal{F}}).

Definition 3.4
.

A
(*,\#,\otimes)
-formalism on
({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}})

satisfies the Voevodsky conditions
if it satisfies the following conditions

•

Homotopy invariance:
for every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
and every vector bundle
p:E\to S
on S, the unit map

{\rm id}\to p_{*}p^{*}

is an equivalence

•

Localization:
for every decomposition of a variety
X
into a closed subvariety and a complementary open

Z\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}X\overset{j}{\hookleftarrow}U

in
{\mathcal{S}}
,
i_{*}
is a fully faithful functor with essential image spanned by objects in the kernel of
j^{*}

•

Thom stability:
for every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
and every locally free sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}
on
S
, the Thom twist endofunctor
\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
is an equivalence on
{\mathbb{D}}(S)

We will also use the term
motivic category over
({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}})
to describe a
(*,\#,\otimes)
-formalism over
({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}})
which satisfies the Voevodsky conditions, since the Voevodsky conditions are equivalent (in the triangulated case) to the conditions of Cisinski-Déglise under which a premotivic category defines a motivic category.

Remark 3.5
.

If
{\mathbb{D}}
is a
(*,\#,\otimes)
-formalism on
{\mathcal{S}}
satisfying the Voevodsky conditions, then the
\infty
-categories
{\mathbb{D}}
are stable.

Note that the features we have already discussed automatically imply some of the most characteristic features of the notion of a six functors formalism, namely, the exceptional operations.

Theorem 3.6
.

Given any finite type morphism
f:{\mathbb{D}}(X)\to{\mathbb{D}}(Y)
, there exists an adjunction

(f_{!}\dashv f^{!}):{\mathbb{D}}(X)\rightleftarrows{\mathbb{D}}(Y)

and a natural transformation
\alpha_{f}:f_{!}\to f_{*}
satisfying the following conditions:

•

There are canonical equivalences
f_{!}\simeq f_{\#}
and
f^{!}\simeq f^{*}
if
f
is an open immersion

•

\alpha_{f}
is an equivalence if
f
is a proper morphism

•

The functor
f_{!}
satisfies base change, or in other words, given a cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
{\mathcal{S}}
, the natural transformations

{\text{Ex}}_{!}^{*}:p^{*}f_{!}\to g_{!}q^{*}\text{ and }{\text{Ex}}_{*}^{!}:q_{*}g^{!}\to f^{!}p_{*}

are equivalences

•

The functor
f_{!}
satisfies the projection formula. In other words,
f_{!}
is a morphism of
{\mathbb{D}}(Y)
-modules, where
{\mathbb{D}}(X)
is regarded as a
{\mathbb{D}}(Y)
module via the symmetric monoidal functor
f^{*}
. Furthermore, the canonical morphisms

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}\otimes f_{!}({\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\to f_{!}(f^{*}({\mathcal{F}})\otimes{\mathcal{G}}),

\displaystyle\underline{{\rm Hom}}(f^{*}({\mathcal{F}}),f^{!}({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\to f^{!}(\underline{{\rm Hom}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})),

\displaystyle f_{*}(\underline{{\rm Hom}}({\mathcal{F}},f^{!}({\mathcal{G}})))

\displaystyle\to\underline{{\rm Hom}}(f_{!}({\mathcal{F}}),{\mathcal{G}})

are equivalences for all
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{D}}(X)
and
{\mathcal{G}}\in{\mathbb{D}}(Y)

Proof.

This is
[
29
]
theorem 2.34.
∎

In fact, if
f
is étale, then the natural map
f^{!}\to f^{*}
is an equivalence.

Before we continue, at this point we should introduce a bit of notation. Suppose that
f:X\to Y
is a morphism of finite type in
{\mathcal{S}}
. By the above, we have adjunctions
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
and
f_{!}\dashv f^{!}
. We will use the notation

•

\eta_{f}^{*}:{\rm id}\to f_{*}f^{*}
and
{\varepsilon}_{f}^{*}:f^{*}f_{*}\to{\rm id}
for the unit and counit of the first adjunction

•

\eta_{f}^{!}:{\rm id}\to f^{!}f_{!}
and
{\varepsilon}_{f}^{!}:f_{!}f^{!}\to{\rm id}
for the unit and counit of the second adjunction

If, in addition,
f
happens to be smooth, we have a third adjunction
f_{\#}\dashv f^{*}
, and we will denote its unit and counit by

\eta_{f}^{\#}:{\rm id}\to f^{*}f_{\#}\text{ and }{\varepsilon}_{f}^{\#}:f_{\#}f^{*}\to{\rm id}

if needed.

Definition 3.7
.

A premotivic category
{\mathbb{D}}
on
({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}})
is
compactly generated
if

•

For every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
, the
\infty
-category
{\mathbb{D}}(S)
is compactly generated

•

For every morphism
f:T\to S
in
{\mathcal{S}}
, the inverse image functor
f^{*}:{\mathbb{D}}(S)\to{\mathbb{D}}(T)
is a compact functor (preserves compact objects)

Definition 3.8
.

Given a premotivic category
{\mathbb{D}}
over
({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}})
, we refer to
{\mathbb{D}}
as
continuous
if for every cofiltered system of affine schemes
(S_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
in
{\mathcal{S}}
with limit
S
, the canonical functor

\varprojlim_{\alpha}{\mathbb{D}}(S_{\alpha})\to{\mathbb{D}}(S)

is an equivalence.

In practice, essentially all of the (pre)motivic categories we encounter will be compactly generated, and many will be continuous as well. There will be more on compact (and in particular constructible) generation towards the end of the section.

Theorem 3.9
.

Suppose that
{\mathbb{D}}
is a motivic category over
({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}})
and that one has a cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
{\mathcal{S}}
. Then we have the following different forms of base change in addition to those discussed before (presented in their most general forms):

•

Proper base change:
If
f
is a proper morphism, then there is a canonical equivalence

{\text{Ex}}_{*}^{*}:p^{*}f_{*}\to g_{*}q^{*}

of functors
{\mathbb{D}}(X)\to{\mathbb{D}}(Y^{\prime})

•

Smooth-proper base change:
If
f
is a proper morphism and
p
and
q
are smooth, then there is a canonical equivalence

{\text{Ex}}_{\#*}:p_{\#}g_{*}\to f_{*}q_{\#}

of functors
{\mathbb{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathbb{D}}(Y)

•

Finite type-smooth base change:
If
f
is of finite type and
p
and
q
are smooth, then there is a natural equivalence

{\text{Ex}}^{*!}:q^{*}f^{!}\to g^{!}p^{*}

of functors
{\mathbb{D}}(Y)\to{\mathbb{D}}(X^{\prime})

•

Finite type-proper base change:
If
f
is of finite type and
p
is proper, then there is a natural equivalence

{\text{Ex}}_{!*}:f_{!}q_{*}\to p_{*}g_{!}

of functors
{\mathbb{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathbb{D}}(Y)

Proof.

This consists of various statements in
[
29
]
theorem 2.24, corollary 2.37, and corollary 2.39.
∎

Really the crux of many of the proofs in the following section will be the various forms of base change we have discussed.

Remark 3.10
.

In what follows, noting that Tate twists generally commute with all of the six operations, we abuse notation by doing things such as writing
f^{*}\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
instead of
\langle f^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\rangle\circ f^{*}
, etc.

Theorem 3.11
.

Consider
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
and two smooth
S
-schemes
p:X\to S
and
q:Y\to S
. Then one has the following two results:

•

Relative purity:
If
X
and
Y
are connected via a closed immersion
i:X\hookrightarrow Y
over
S
, then there is a canonical isomorphism

q_{\#}i_{*}\simeq p_{\#}\langle{\mathcal{N}}_{X/Y}\rangle,

with
{\mathcal{N}}_{X/Y}
the conormal sheaf of
i

•

If
f:X\to Y
is an unrammified morphism over
S
, then there is a canonical isomorphism

f^{!}q^{*}\simeq p^{*}\langle{\mathcal{L}}_{X/Y}\rangle

where
{\mathcal{L}}_{X/Y}
is the relative cotangent complex of
f

Proof.

This is
[
29
]
theorem 2.25 and 2.43.
∎

In particular, these can be used to conclude two important results.

Theorem 3.12
.

Atiyah duality:
If
f:X\to Y
in
{\mathcal{S}}
is smooth and proper, then one has a canonical morphism of functors

{\varepsilon}_{f}:f_{\#}\langle{\mathcal{L}}_{f}\rangle\to f_{*},

where
{\mathcal{L}}_{f}
is the cotangent complex of
f
.

Proof.

This is
[
29
]
theorem 2.24 (iii).
∎

This theorem is very interesting, as it implies, among other things, that the left and right adjoints of
f^{*}
are related by a Thom twist when
f
is smooth and proper.

Theorem 3.13
.

Purity:
If
{\mathbb{D}}
is a motivic category over
{\mathcal{S}}
, then for any smooth morphism
f:X\to Y
, one has a canonical equivalence

\text{pur}_{f}:f^{!}\to f^{*}\langle{\mathcal{L}}_{f}\rangle

of functors
{\mathbb{D}}(Y)\to{\mathbb{D}}(X)
, thus generalizing our previous result on étale morphisms from before.

Proof.

This is
[
29
]
theorem 2.44.
∎

We will also make very heavy use of the following consequence of localization.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
{\mathbb{D}}
be a premotivic category on
{\mathcal{S}}
, and suppose that one has a closed immersion
i
with open complement
j
of the form

Z\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}X\overset{j}{\hookleftarrow}U.

Then the functors
j_{*}
and
j_{\#}
are fully faithful and furthermore one has
j^{*}i_{*}\simeq 0
and
i^{*}j_{\#}\simeq 0
. If, furthermore
{\mathbb{D}}
satisfies the localization property, then one has the canonical cofiber sequences

j_{\#}j^{*}\to{\rm id}\to i_{*}i^{*}\text{ and }i_{*}i^{!}\to{\rm id}\to j_{*}j^{*}

(there is a way to define
i^{!}
in this case without assuming that
{\mathbb{D}}
is motivic, simply as the homotopy fiber of
i^{*}\to i^{*}j_{*}j^{*}
). If, in fact,
{\mathbb{D}}
happens to be motivic, then the above cofiber sequences are equivalent to

j_{!}j^{!}\to{\rm id}\to i_{*}i^{*}\text{ and }i_{!}i^{!}\to{\rm id}\to j_{*}j^{*}.

Proof.

This is
[
29
]
remark 2.9
∎

We now come to a very important definition. The subcategory of constructible objects will be what allows us to extract meaningful K-theory from our six functors formalism and not fall prey to swindles.

Definition 3.15
.

Consider a motivic category
{\mathbb{D}}
over
{\mathcal{S}}
and a scheme
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
. For any
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\mathbb{D}}(S)
, we say that
{\mathcal{F}}
is
constructible
if it satisfies one of the following equivalent conditions:

•

{\mathcal{F}}
lies in the thick subcategory generated by
f_{\#}f^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{S})\langle-n\rangle
(or
f_{!}f^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})\langle-n\rangle
via purity) with
f:X\to S
a smooth morphism of finite presentation and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}

•

{\mathcal{F}}
lies in the thick subcategory generated by
f_{\#}f^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{S})\langle-n\rangle
(or
f_{!}f^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})\langle-n\rangle
via purity) with
f:X\to S
a smooth morphism of finite presentation with
X
affine and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}

The
subcategory of constructible objects over
S
is denoted by
{\mathbb{D}}_{\text{cons}}(S)\subseteq{\mathbb{D}}(S)
.

Since it is this subcategory of constructible objects that we will need to extract meaningful K-theory in what follows, we will list here several of its important properties.

Definition 3.16
.

We say that a motivic category
{\mathbb{D}}
is constructibly generated if it is compactly generated and every constructible object is compact. It then follows that the compactness is equivalent to constructibility (
[
29
]
definition 2.5.5).

Proposition 3.17
.

The property of constructibility in
{\mathbb{D}}
is stable under

•

Tensor product with any constructible object

•

Inverse image along any morphism in
{\mathcal{S}}

•

\#
-direct image along any finitely presented smooth morphism in
{\mathcal{S}}

•

Thom twist by a perfect complex

•

Exceptional direct image along any finite type morphism in
{\mathcal{S}}

Proof.

The first three statements comprise
[
29
]
proposition 2.57, the third comprises
[
29
]
proposition 2.60, and the fourth comprises
[
29
]
theorem 2.61.
∎

This implies that
{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{\text{cons}}
is a presheaf of symmetric monoidal essentially small
\infty
-categories on
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Corollary 3.18
.

If
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
is a closed immersion, the property of constructibility in
{\mathbb{D}}(X)
is stable under the endofunctor
i_{*}i^{*}
.

Proof.

This is
[
29
]
corollary 2.58.
∎

3.2.
Some Examples of Motivic
\infty
-Categories

Perhaps the canonical example of a motivic
\infty
-category over
{\mathcal{S}}
is the stable motivic homotopy category
SH
. We will go through the definition here. We will fix a subcategory
{\mathcal{S}}
of schemes over some base
B
and an admissible subcategory
{\mathcal{A}}
of
{\textbf{Sch}}_{B}
for the rest of this section. We require that
{\mathcal{A}}
satisfy the following properties:

•

{\mathcal{A}}
is an essentially small full subcategory of
{\textbf{Sch}}_{B}

•

For every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
, one has that the over category
{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}
contains
S

•

If
X\in{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}
and
Y
is étale and of finite presentation over
S
, then
Y\in{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}

•

If
X\in{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}
, then
X\times{\mathbb{A}}^{1}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}

•

If
X,Y\in{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}
, then
X\times_{S}Y\in{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}

Note that we do not necessarily require
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
in this section (in fact, our typical choice for
{\mathcal{A}}
will merely be
{\textbf{Sm}}_{B}
).

We construct the stable motivic homotopy category in several steps. The first order of business is to construct the
unstable motivic homotopy category

\textbf{H}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
for any
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

Definition 3.19
.

Given any
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
, an
{\mathcal{A}}
-fibered space over
S
is a presheaf on
{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}
with values in a suitable category of spaces
S
, such as Kan complexes. We denote the
\infty
-category of such presheaves as
{\textbf{PrSh}}_{\textbf{S}}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
.

•

An element
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\textbf{PrSh}}_{\textbf{S}}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})

satisfies Nisnevich descent
if it satisfies C̆ech descent with respect to the restriction of the Nisnevich topology restricted to
{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}
. In other words, for all
X\in{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}
(suppressing structure morphism) and all Nisnevich coverings
\{U_{\alpha}\to X\}_{\alpha}
, the diagram

{\mathcal{F}}(X)\to\prod_{\alpha}{\mathcal{F}}(U_{\alpha})\rightrightarrows\prod_{\beta,\gamma}{\mathcal{F}}(U_{\beta}\times_{X}U_{\gamma})\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}\cdots

exibits
{\mathcal{F}}(X)
as its homotopy limit

•

An element
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\textbf{PrSh}}_{\textbf{S}}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})

is
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy invariant
if for any
X\in{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}
, the natural map

{\mathcal{F}}(X)\to{\mathcal{F}}(X\times_{S}{\mathbb{A}}^{1})

is an equivalence

An element
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\textbf{PrSh}}_{\textbf{S}}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
is referred to as
motivic
if it satisfies Nisnevich descent and
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy invariance. The category of motivic
{\mathcal{A}}
-fibered spaces is referred to as the
unstable motivic homotopy category over S with respect to
{\mathcal{A}}
and is denoted
\textbf{H}({\mathcal{A}}_{S})
. It may equally be seen as the successive left Bousfeld localization of
{\textbf{PrSh}}_{\textbf{S}}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
at the classes of morphisms
{\mathcal{F}}(X)\to\text{Tot}(\{U_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{F}})
for any
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\textbf{PrSh}}_{\textbf{S}}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
, any
X\in{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}
, and any Nisnevich cover
\{U_{\alpha}\to X\}_{\alpha}
(here
\text{Tot}(\{U_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{F}})
is the totalization of the C̆ech complex of
{\mathcal{F}}
at our Nisnevich cover) and
{\mathcal{F}}(X)\to{\mathcal{F}}(X\times_{S}{\mathbb{A}}^{1})
for any
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\textbf{PrSh}}_{\textbf{S}}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
and any
X\in{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}
. This classification is often more useful when working with
\textbf{H}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
.

We will let
\textbf{H}(S)
denote the above category when
{\mathcal{A}}_{/S}={\textbf{Sm}}_{S}
, or in other words
{\mathcal{A}}={\textbf{Sm}}_{B}
for our base scheme
B
.

It should be noted that
\textbf{H}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
is symmetric monoidal via the cartesian product.

Now, we are almost at our desired definition. In particular, we let
\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
refer to the pointed objects in
\textbf{H}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
. The corresponding symmetric monoidal structure on
\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
is induced by
\wedge
. The forgetful functor
\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})\to\textbf{H}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
has a symmetric monoidal left adjoint given by taking a free disjoint baseboint
{\mathcal{F}}\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}_{+}
.

Definition 3.20
.

Given a vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
on
S
with total space
E\to S
, we define the
Thom space

\text{Th}_{S}({\mathcal{E}})
to be the cofiber of the inclusion
E\setminus S\hookrightarrow E
. Note that this is naturally an element of
\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
and that it must be compact.

Denoting
{\mathbb{T}}_{S}:=\text{Th}_{S}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})={\mathbb{A}}^{1}/({\mathbb{A}}^{1}-S)
, we obtain suspension endofunctor

\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}:={\mathbb{T}}_{S}\wedge(-):\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})\to\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S}).

This has a right adjoint loop space functor

\Omega_{\mathbb{T}}:\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})\to\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S}).

Now, we are ready to introduce our main definition.

Definition 3.21
.

Given
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
, we may define the
motivic stable homotopy category over
S
relative to
{\mathcal{A}}
, denoted
{\textbf{SH}}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
, to be the cofiltered homotopy colimit of

\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})\overset{\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}}{\to}\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})\overset{\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}}{\to}\cdots

taken in the
(\infty,2)
-category of presentable
\infty
-categories with left-adjoint functors or equivalently as the filtered homotopy colimit of

\cdots\overset{\Omega_{\mathbb{T}}}{\to}\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})\overset{\Omega_{\mathbb{T}}}{\to}\textbf{H}_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})

in either the
(\infty,2)
-category of presentable
\infty
-categories with right adjoint functors, or just in the
(\infty,2)
-category of
\infty
-categories. An element of
{\textbf{SH}}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
will be referred to as an
{\mathcal{A}}
-fibered spectrum over
S
.

This motivic stable homotopy category, originally defined by Voevodsky and Morel, has many nice properties, among them that it satisfies a six functors formalism. When
{\mathcal{A}}
is not specified, as above, we assume that we are dealing with the subcategory of all smooth morphisms in
{\mathcal{S}}
, and merely use the notation
{\textbf{SH}}(S)
, referring to it as
the motivic stable homotopy category over
S
.

It should be noted that
{\textbf{SH}}({\mathcal{A}}_{/S})
has a natural symmetric monoidal structure, denoted
\otimes
, with the unit denoted
\mathbf{1}_{S}
.

Theorem 3.22
.

The motivic stable homotopy category defines a presheaf of stable
\infty
-categories
{\textbf{SH}}^{*}:{\mathcal{S}}^{op}\to{\textbf{Cat}}_{\infty}^{stab}
. This presheaf naturally descends to the structure of a motivic
\infty
-category.

Proof.

This is essentially the entire first half of
[
29
]
.
∎

For a deeper dive into the understanding of the
\infty
-categorical structure of the stable motivic homotopy category, the reader is encouraged to consult Marc Hoyois’ phenomenal paper
[
28
]
, as well as
[
29
]
.

An important category related to the motivic stable homotopy category is its rationalization, denoted
{\textbf{SH}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(S):={\textbf{SH}}(S)\otimes{\mathcal{D}}({\mathbb{Q}})
, where the tensor product is taken in the
\infty
-category of stable
\infty
-categories. In addition to
{\textbf{SH}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
being a motivic
\infty
-category, it also satisfies several other nice properties that will be relevant for us in our upcoming examples. Among these nice properties are the following.

Theorem 3.23
.

The rationalization of the motivic stable homotopy category
{\textbf{SH}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
satisfies the following properties:

•

Absolute Purity:
for any morphism
f:X\to S
of Noetherian schemes which is factorizable through a closed immersion and a smooth morphism, one obtains an equivalence

\mathbf{1}_{X}\langle d\rangle\simeq f^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})

in
{\textbf{SH}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
, where
d={\rm rank}(T_{X/S})
is the virtual dimension of
f

•

Finiteness:
over quasi-excellent scheme, the six functors preserve constructibility

•

Duality:
for every separated morphism of finite type
f:X\to S
with
S
quasi-excellent and regular, one has that
f^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})
is a dualizing object in
{\textbf{SH}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)

Proof.

This is
[
18
]
corollary C parts II-IV.
∎

In addition,
{\textbf{SH}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\simeq\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{1}}(-,{\mathbb{Q}})
, where
\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{1}}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
is the same stabilization applied to
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-local complexes of sheaves of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-vector spaces satisfying Nisnevich descent.

One of our most important examples will be the category of Beilinson motives
{\textbf{DM}}_{B}
, which is defined as the subcategory of modules over the motivic ring spectrum
\textbf{H}_{B}
for varying
S
. This will in particular be our primary example, and discussion of it will occupy much of a later section. It should be noted that
{\textbf{DM}}_{B}
satisfies the same nice properties discussed above.

Before one gets the idea that all motivic categories comprise ”categories of motives” or ”categories of motivic spaces” of some form, let us introduce two related examples decidedly less motivic in nature.

Definition 3.24
.

Given some scheme
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
, we define
\textbf{D}_{\acute{e}t}(S,{\mathbb{Z}}/l{\mathbb{Z}})
to be the stable derived
\infty
-category of étale sheaves valued in
{\mathbb{Z}}/l{\mathbb{Z}}
for some
l
coprime to the exponential characteristic of our base scheme. Further define
\textbf{D}(S,{\mathbb{Z}}_{l})
to be the stable derived
\infty
-category of
l
-adic sheaves on
S
.

It has been known since SGA4 and the work of Deligne
[
19
]
that these two categories are motivic at least on the level of triangulated homotopy categories, and more recent work has demonstrated that both of them define motivic
\infty
-categories as well (see, for example,
[
25
]
or
[
24
]
).

Note that one example we have NOT listed is that of Voevodsky motives. It is still open whether or not Voevodsky’s derived (stable
\infty-
)category of motives
{\textbf{DM}}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
over a scheme
S
defines a motivic category in the sense we have described above. It is certainly true if one restricts to particular choices of
{\mathcal{S}}
. For example, as we will discuss later, for any excellent geometrically unibranch base scheme
S
, one has that
{\textbf{DM}}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq{\textbf{DM}}_{B}(S)
. That said, it remains an open problem whether or not Voevodsky’s category of motives satisfies a six functors formalism more generally.

3.3.
Constructing Derived Motivic Measures from Six Functors Formalisms

The purpose of this section is to demonstrate the existence of a procedure for converting generalized six functors formalisms into derived motivic measures. To do this, we will begin by proving identities about the four functors, and ultimately evaluate everything at the tensor unit over some base, yielding a weakly W-exact map that descends to our desired motivic measure.

Proposition 3.25
.

Given any commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

of varieties, one has that the triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{!}h^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{!}g^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm id}}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm id}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{*}g^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{*}h^{*}}

commute. The dual triangles for (co)units of the appropriate adjunctions also commute.

Proof.

This statement is basically a specialization to the setting of the six functors formalism of the fact that
\infty
-categorical adjunctions compose (cf.
[
41
]
).
∎

Corollary 3.26
.

Given a composition of closed immersions of
S
-schemes

Proof.

This follows pretty directly from the preceding lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.27
.

Suppose one has an adjunction of cospans of
\infty
-categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

(note that the above diagram is not commutative as such; rather, it is commutative if one direction of vertical morphisms is omitted, but is presented above to highlight the adjointness of the different vertical morphisms). Then the induced morphisms on pullbacks in both directions form an adjunction

Proof.

The crux of this proof will be to define the units and counits for our hypothesized adjunction and to demonstrate that they satisfy the triangle identities. Let us take
\eta,\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}
and
{\varepsilon},{\varepsilon}^{\prime},{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}
to be the unit and counit of the middle, right, and left adjunctions, respectively. Note that we get the isomorphisms

(\delta\times_{\beta}\psi)\circ(\gamma\times_{\alpha}{\varphi})\simeq(\delta\circ\gamma)\times_{(\beta\circ\alpha)}(\psi\circ{\varphi})

and

(\gamma\times_{\alpha}{\varphi})\circ(\delta\times_{\beta}\psi)\simeq(\gamma\circ\delta)\times_{(\alpha\circ\beta)}({\varphi}\circ\psi)

due to the natural commutativity of diagrams such as this one:

and
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\times_{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}
back along the equivalence. All that remains is to show that these two satisfy the triangle identities, but this just follows directly from the triangle identities for each of the units and counits of the adjunctions in the cospan above.

\infty
-categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

\infty

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Proof.

The adjunction of cospans in the lemma description yields a related adjunction of cospans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\Delta[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\times D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\Delta[1]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\times q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\times p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{1},p_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\Delta[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\times t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\times h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\times s}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{1},p_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\Delta[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Now, note that by definition, one has that the pullbacks of these cospans are the appropriate comma
\infty
-categories, so we obtain our desired adjunction.
∎

with
i
and
i^{\prime}
closed immersions and
j
and
j^{\prime}
open immersions. Then one has that the triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}_{!}i^{\prime!}j^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{*}i_{!}i^{!}j^{\prime*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varepsilon}_{i^{\prime}}^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varepsilon}_{i}^{!}}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}_{!}i^{\prime!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}_{!}j_{*}j^{*}i^{\prime!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime*}i^{\prime}_{!}i^{\prime!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{j}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{j^{\prime}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

homotopy commute.

Proof.

This proposition can be proven by showing that it is true for each relevant component of the units and counits involved. We start with the shriek units. Note that given any
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\mathbb{D}}(W)
, we have the following adjunction of cospans of

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where the commutativity of the upwards-oriented lefthand square is the result of base change, and the commutativity of the upwards-oriented righthand square is the result of the triangle equivalence (the downwards-oriented squares are more immediately commutative). This implies that the induced functor
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbb{D}}(Z)}(i_{!},j^{\prime*}{\mathcal{F}})\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbb{D}}(W)}(i^{\prime}_{!},j^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime*}{\mathcal{F}})
is a right adjoint, and thus preserves limits. In particular, it preserves terminal objects, which shows that for any
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\mathbb{D}}(W)
, the first triangle commutes, and thus the triangle commutes in the category of functors. The second proof is dual to the first.
∎

where the vertical morphisms are simply the homotopy-compatible morphisms. Furthermore, considered as an
\infty
-morphism of commutative squares, the diagram above is invertible.

Proof.

This proof will make heavy use of the fact that given an
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
, the functor
{\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\Delta[1]})\to{\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}})^{\Delta[1]}
is surjective on objects, full, and conservative (
[
41
]
3.1.1). In particular, this further implies that for any
n
,
{\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\Delta[1]^{n}})\to{\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}})^{\Delta[1]^{n}}
is surjective on objects, full, and conservative as well.

Now, consider the category of cubes
{\mathcal{C}}^{\Delta[1]^{3}}\simeq({\mathcal{C}}^{\Delta[1]})^{\Delta[1]^{2}}\simeq({\mathcal{C}}^{\Delta[1]^{2}})^{\Delta[1]}
. The primary characterization of cubes that we will make use of is as arrows in commutative squares, or the middle term in the above string of equivalences. By our assumptions, we see that the cube written in the lemma description above commutes when considered in the homotopy category
({\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}})^{\Delta[1]^{2}})^{\Delta[1]}
. Since the canonical functor
{\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\Delta[1]^{3}})\to{\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}})^{\Delta[1]^{3}}
is surjective on objects, one has that there must exist a commutative diagram
D\in{\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\Delta[1]^{3}})
(in other words, an object of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\Delta[1]^{3}}
) filling the commutative cube, which settles the first part of the lemma.

Let
J
denote the interval groupoid and consider an inclusion
\Delta[1]\hookrightarrow J
. This induces for any
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
a map
{\mathcal{C}}^{J}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\Delta[1]}
whose essential image is the subcategory of equivalences in
{\mathcal{C}}
. Furthermore, the map from
{\mathcal{C}}^{J}
onto its essential image is essentially surjective, as a morphism in an
\infty
-category is an equivalence if and only if it can be lifted to a map from
J
to
{\mathcal{C}}
. (Indeed, it is surjective on objects, in spite of this not being a homotopy-invariant notion.) In particular, on
1
-categories, this is the inclusion of a subcategory since inverses to equivalences are uniquely defined in
1
-categories. Now, we have the commutative square

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The bottom morphism is conservative by [R-V]. We wish to show that it is surjective on objects as well. Note that both the left and right vertical morphism are surjective on objects. Note that the vertical morphisms precisely describe those morphisms, either in the underlying
\infty
-category or in the homotopy category, which are invertible. Furthermore, the map restricted to these subcategories must also be surjective on objects. Hence, one must have that

{\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}}^{J})\to{\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}})^{J}

is surjective on objects as well. Now, we note that there is a string of morphisms

{\textbf{Ho}}(({\mathcal{C}}^{J})^{\Delta[1]^{2}})\to{\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}}^{J})^{\Delta[1]^{2}}\to({\textbf{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}})^{J})^{\Delta[1]^{2}}

which are surjective on objects. Hence, it must be the case that our cube above admits a filling to
({\mathcal{C}}^{J})^{\Delta[1]^{2}}
. Furthermore, since
({\mathcal{C}}^{J})^{\Delta[1]^{2}}\simeq({\mathcal{C}}^{\Delta[1]^{2}})^{J}
, our cube fills to an equivalence of commutative squares in
{\mathcal{C}}
. Finally, this filling must be unique up to a contractible choice since all of this is done ambiently in the
(\infty,2)
-category of
\infty
-categories.
∎

.
